The sentence uses the vernacular "part of the population in", which is ambiguous between touches and overlaps. Follow the detailed rules below, especially the directional check and bbox caution. Do not guess.

Vernacular-to-Topology Reference:
  WITHIN    — "is in", "located in", "part of"              (A is fully inside B)
  CONTAINS  — "is home to", "includes", "hosts"             (A fully encloses B)
  TOUCHES   — "borders", "adjacent to", "enclave of",
              "surrounded by" (partial), "across from" (water boundary)
  CROSSES   — "passes through", "along", "traverses",
              "at the mouth of" / "upstream from" (full LineString)
  OVERLAPS  — "partly in", "extends into", "straddles",
              "part of population in", "county seat of" (may extend into adjacent counties)
  DISJOINT  — "far from", "miles away", "separate from" (large distance)

Ambiguous vernacular (read carefully):
  "county seat of" → does NOT imply within — the city may extend into adjacent counties → overlaps
  "suburb of"      → does NOT imply touches — the suburb may be separated from the main city → disjoint
  "surrounded by"  → may be touches (partial encirclement), not within (full containment)
  "enclave of"     → touches (shared boundary), not within (interior)
  "across from"    → touches (water boundary), not within
  "most sides"     → touches (partial), not within
  "along" + river/road → likely crosses (full LineString traversal), not just touches

Rules:
1. DIRECTIONAL CHECK (mandatory before answering):
   — "contains" means A encloses B; "within" means A is inside B.
   — Ask: does this predicate apply A→B or B→A?
   — If the data shows B encloses A, the answer is "within", NOT "contains".

2. TEXTUAL QUALIFIER PRIORITY (override bbox signals when contradictory):
   — "most sides", "surrounded by" with incomplete encirclement → touches.
   — "partly in", "extends into", "part of population in" → overlaps.
   — "enclave of" → touches (shared boundary, not interior).
   — "across from" over water → touches (water boundary is shared in GIS).
   — "county seat of" alone does NOT imply within.

3. GEOMETRY TYPE CONSTRAINTS:
   — Polygon vs Polygon: possible relationships — within, contains, touches, overlaps, disjoint.
   — Two Polygons partially sharing area → overlaps.
   — If A is entirely inside B → within.  If B is inside A → contains.
   — Use bbox as a clue, not proof.

4. BBOX CAUTION (never conclude directly from bbox):
   — bbox overlap ≠ polygon overlap. Polygons inside overlapping bboxes may still be disjoint or only touching.
   — bbox containment ≠ within/contains. A country bbox contains almost all nearby entities.
   — Treat bbox as a hint, never as definitive evidence.

5. GAP TOLERANCE:
   — A bbox gap of < 0.01 degrees (~1 km) is NOT proof of disjoint.
   — Administrative boundaries often share exact edges with 0-meter gap.

6. ENTITY VALIDATION:
   — Verify the OSM entity matches the expected state/region.
   — If the entity location does not align with the context, treat its geometry as unreliable.

7. VERNACULAR PRIORITY (ambiguous cases):
   — "part of the population in" is ambiguous: could be touches or overlaps.
   — Do NOT default to within or contains based on entity type or order.
   — Use the textual cue, bbox hints, and geometry (if valid) to decide.

8. 3-STEP CHECK for OVERLAPS (do this before concluding within or touches):
   Step 1: Is A completely inside B? -> within (not overlaps)
   Step 2: Is B completely inside A? -> contains (not overlaps)
   Step 3: Do A and B share SOME area but NEITHER fully contains the other? -> overlaps
   Strong overlaps signals: "partly in", "extends into", "straddles",
                            "part

  [ToT] ✅ FINAL PREDICTION: within

# Vernacular: "county seat of"
# Valid predicates: contains, within, touches, crosses, disjoint, overlaps

# 1. DIRECTION CHECK (mandatory before answering)
#   - "contains" means A encloses B; "within" means A is inside B.
#   - Ask: does this predicate apply A->B or B->A?
#   - If data shows B encloses A, answer is "within", not "contains".

# 2. TEXTUAL QUALIFIER PRIORITY (override geometric signals when contradictory)
#   - "county seat of" alone does NOT imply within.
#   - Strong vernacular for overlaps: "extends into", "partly in", "straddles",
#     "part of population in", "county seat of" (city crosses county line).
#   - If vernacular contradicts bbox, use the textual qualifier.

# 3. GEOMETRY TYPE CONSTRAINTS (Polygon vs Polygon)
#   - No LineString or Point involved, so crosses/touches are ruled out.
#   - Two Polygons partially sharing area -> overlaps (must have some interior intersection).
#   - If A were inside B -> within; if B inside A -> contains.

# 4. BBOX CLUE (never conclude directly from bbox)
#   - A's bbox inside B's bbox is a hint, not proof.
#   - Use bbox as a clue, but rely on polygon geometry.

# 5. ENTITY VALIDATION (use coordinates, bbox, and hierarchy)

#   - Both are valid city and county polygons, no mismatch warnings.

# 6. RANK RELATIONSHIP (use distance + bbox overlap)

#   - No strong textual or directional evidence for within or

  [ToT] ── BRANCHES_RAW ──
Does this predicate apply A→B or B→A? (Answer with "A" or "B")
--- Reasoning Branches ---
1. Direction + Textual Qualifier
2. Geometry (bbox + polygon)
3. Ambiguous / Edge Case
Only one predicate may be correct. End with: Answer: [predicate]"

We are an expert in geospatial topological reasoning. Use the coordinates, bounding boxes, and hierarchy below to deduce the vernacular spatial relation. Do not guess.

Vernacular-to-Topology Reference:
  WITHIN    — "is in", "located in", "part of"              (A is fully inside B)
  CONTAINS  — "contains", "home to", "includes"             (A fully encloses B)
  TOUCHES   — "borders", "adjacent to", "enclave of", "surrounded by" (partial, not full containment)
  CROSSES   — "passes through", "along", "traverses"        (full LineString through Polygon)
  OVERLAPS  — "partly in", "extends into", "straddles", "county seat of" (may cross into adjacent counties)
  DISJOINT  — "far from", "miles away", "separate from"     (no shared points)

Reference list of predicates (exactly one must be chosen):
  contains, within, touches, crosses, disjoint, overlaps

Rules (follow ALL steps, never skip one):
1. DIRECTION CHECK (mandatory before answering):
   - "contains" means A encloses B; "within" means A is inside B.
   - Explicitly ask: does this verb apply A→B or B→A?
   - If the data shows B encloses A, the answer is "within", NOT "contains".

2. TEXTUAL QUALIFIER PRIORITY (override bbox signals when contradictory):
   - "most sides", "surrounded by" with incomplete encirclement → touches.
   - "partly in", "extends into", "part of population in" → overlaps.
   - "enclave of" → touches (shared boundary, not interior).
   - "across from" over water → touches (water boundary is shared in GIS).
   - "county seat of" alone does NOT imply within.

3. GEOMETRY TYPE CONSTRAINTS:
   - LineString (river, road) traversing a Polygon interior → crosses.
   - LineString touching only Polygon boundary → touches.
   - "At the mouth of" / "upstream from" → check bbox + polygon; may be touches or within.
   - Two Polygons partially sharing area → overlaps.

4. BOUNDING BOX CAUTION (never conclude directly from bbox):
   - bbox overlap ≠ polygon overlap.
   - bbox containment ≠ within/contains.
   - Treat bbox as a clue, never as proof.

5. ENTITY VALIDATION (warnings):
   - If the entity's coordinates/bbox do NOT match the context, its geometry may be unreliable.
   - Do NOT use bbox or centroid alone to decide the relation.

6. VERNACULAR CLUES (prioritize over bbox geometry):
   - "include" (plural) → A contains B (verb first, noun second).
   - "suburb of" (first entity is the suburb, second is the city) → overlaps.
   - "most sides", "surrounded by" (partial encirclement) → touches.
   - "at the mouth of", "upstream from" (river) → touches (shared water boundary).
   - "county seat of" (city may extend into adjacent counties) → overlaps.

7. DISTANCE CHECK (bbox centroid distance is a clue, not proof):
   - A moderate distance (1-5 km) is consistent with within, touches, or overlaps.
   - A large distance (> 5 km) strongly suggests disjoint.
   - Do NOT decide the relation based solely on centroid distance.

8. 3-STEP CHECK for OVERLAPS (do this BEFORE concluding within or touches):
   Step 1: Is A completely inside B? -> within (not overlaps)
   Step 2: Is B completely inside A? -> contains (not overlaps)
   Step 3: Do A and B share SOME area but NEITHER fully contains the other? -> overlaps
   Strong overlaps signals: "extends into", "partly in", "straddles",
                            "part of population in", "county seat of" (city crosses county line).

9. Pick EXACTLY ONE predicate from: contains, within, touches, crosses, disjoint, overlaps.
10. End with: Answer: [predicate]"

We are an expert in geospatial topological reasoning. 
Vernacular-to-Topology Reference: 
  WITHIN    — "is in", "located in", "part of"              (A is fully inside B) 
  CONTAINS  — "home to", "includes", "location of"          (A fully encloses B) 
  TOUCHES   — "borders", "adjacent to", "enclave of", "surrounded by" (partial) 
  CROSSES   — "passes through", "along", "at the mouth of" / "upstream from" (full LineString) 
  DISJOINT  — "far from", "miles away", "separate from" (large distance) 
  OVERLAPS  — "partly in", "extends into", "straddles", "county seat of" (may cross boundary) 
--- 
CRITICAL DISTINCTIONS (most confused predicates) ---
touches vs overlaps:
  touches  → A and B share ONLY their boundary. Interiors do NOT intersect.
             Example: two adjacent counties sharing a border line.
  overlaps → A and B share SOME interior area. Part of A is inside B, part is outside.
             Example: a city that partially crosses a county boundary.
touches vs crosses:
  touches  → boundary contact only, no traversal through interior.
  crosses  → A passes THROUGH B, entering AND exiting its interior. Requires different geometry dimensions (LineString through Polygon). Example: a highway that enters and exits a city polygon.
contains vs within (DIRECTION IS CRITICAL):
  contains → A is the CONTAINER. B is fully INSIDE A. Ask: "does A surround B?"
  within   → A is INSIDE B. Ask: "is A surrounded by B?"
  KEY RULE: Identify which entity is the enclosing one.
     If A is smaller than B -> "within".  If A is larger and encloses B -> "contains".
     Signals for contains: "home to", "location of", "includes".
     Signals for within: "located in", "in", "part of", "county seat of".
  Do NOT default to "within" just because A is a sub-entity type or mentioned first. 
  Anti-bias: If A is smaller than B, do NOT say "contains" — it is "within". 
  If the vernacular suggests adjacency but the bbox indicates containment, the relation is likely "within" (not "contains"). 
  If the vernacular suggests containment but the bbox indicates disjoint, the relation is likely "within" (not "disjoint"). 
  If the vernacular suggests adjacency but the bbox indicates containment, the relation is likely "within" (not "contains"). 
  If the vernacular suggests containment but the bbox indicates disjoint, the relation is likely "within" (not "disjoint"). 
  If the vernacular suggests adjacency but the bbox indicates containment, the relation is likely "within" (not "contains").

Vernacular-to-Topology Reference:
  WITHIN    — city inside county, state, etc. (A is fully inside B)
  CONTAINS  — city encloses smaller entity (A is the larger, B is inside A)

1. DIRECTION CHECK (mandatory before answering):
   - "suburb of" indicates A is the suburb, B is the main city.

   - "home to" / "located in" -> within (city interior contains the entity).
   - "along" + river/road -> crosses (LineString traverses Polygon interior).

   Step 1: Is A completely inside B? -> within (not overlaps)
   Step 2: Is B completely inside A? -> contains (not overlaps)

    llm=llm,

def get_topology(sentence: str, entity_a: str, entity_b: str, entity_a_type: str, entity_b_type: str, entity_a_polygon: str, entity_b_polygon: str, entity_a_bbox: str, entity_b_bbox: str, entity_a_state: str, entity_b_state: str, verbose: bool = False) -> str:
    """
    Determine the topological relationship between entity_a and entity_b using the provided sentence and OSM geometry data.

    Parameters
    ----------
    sentence : str
        The sentence containing both entities.
    entity_a : str
        Name of the first entity (appears first in the sentence).
    entity_b : str
        Name of the second entity (appears second in the sentence).
    entity_a_type : str
        Entity type of entity_a (e.g., 'city', 'village', 'neighborhood', etc.).
    entity_b_type : str
        Entity type of entity_b.
    entity_a_polygon : str
        WKT Polygon geometry for entity_a (or None if unavailable).
    entity_b_polygon : str
        WKT Polygon geometry for entity_b (or None if unavailable).
    entity_a_bbox : str
        WKT Polygon bbox for entity_a (or None if unavailable).
    entity_b_bbox : str
        WKT Polygon bbox for entity_b (or None if unavailable).
    entity_a_state : str
        State or region of entity_a (or None if unavailable).
    entity_b_state : str
        State or region of entity_b (or None if unavailable).
    verbose : bool, optional
        If True, print detailed reasoning for each branch.

    Returns
    -------
    str
        The chosen topological predicate from: contains, within, touches, crosses, disjoint, overlaps.

    Notes
    -----
    1. Directional CHECK (mandatory):
       - "contains" means A encloses B; "within" means A is inside B.
       - Explicitly ask: does this predicate apply A->B or B->A?
       - If the data shows B encloses A, the answer is "within", NOT "contains".

    2. Textual qualifier priority (override bbox signals when contradictory):
       - "most sides", "surrounded by" with incomplete encirclement -> touches.
       - "partly in", "extends into", "part of population in" -> overlaps.
       - "enclave of" alone does NOT imply within (city may cross county line) -> overlaps.
       - "suburb of" alone does NOT imply touches — may be disjoint.
       - "along" + river/road -> likely crosses (full LineString), not just touches.

    3. Geometry type constraints:
       - LineString traversing Polygon interior -> crosses.
       - LineString touching only Polygon boundary -> touches.
       - "At the mouth of" / "upstream from" -> check bbox and polygon; may be touches or disjoint.
       - Two Polygons partially sharing area -> overlaps.

    4. Bounding box caution (never conclude directly from bbox):
       - bbox overlap ≠ polygon overlap.
       - bbox containment ≠ within/contains (administrative boundaries often share exact edges).
       - Treat bbox as a clue, not proof.

    5. Pick EXACTLY ONE predicate from: contains, within, touches, crosses, disjoint, overlaps.

    6. End with: Answer: [predicate]
    """
    # 1. Directional CHECK (mandatory)
    #   - "contains" means A encloses B; "within" means A is inside B.
    #   - Explicitly ask: does this predicate apply A->B or B->A?
    #   - If the data shows B encloses A, the answer is "within", NOT "contains".

    # 2. Textual qualifier priority (override bbox signals when contradictory)
    #   - "enclave of" alone does NOT imply within (city may cross county line) -> overlaps.
    #   - "suburb of" alone does NOT imply touches — may be disjoint.
    #   - "along" + river/road -> likely crosses (full LineString), not just touches.

    # 3. Geometry type constraints (use WKT if available, otherwise bbox)
    #   - Polygon vs MultiPolygon: use bbox if polygons are separate.
    #   - LineString (river, road) crossing Polygon interior -> crosses.
    #

  [ToT] ✅ FINAL PREDICTION: within

We are ChatGPT, an expert in geospatial topological reasoning.

Vernacular-to-Topology Reference:
  WITHIN    — "is in", "located in", "part of"              (A is fully inside B)
  CONTAINS  — "is home to", "includes", "hosts"             (A fully encloses B)
  TOUCHES   — "borders", "adjacent to", "across from" (water boundary)  (partial, no interior overlap)
  CROSSES   — "passes through", "along", "at the mouth of" / "upstream from" (full LineString)
  OVERLAPS  — "partly in", "extends into", "straddles", "county seat of" (partial area overlap)
  DISJOINT  — "far from", "miles away", "separate from" (confirmed large distance)

Ambiguous vernacular (read carefully):
  "county seat of" → does NOT imply within — the city may extend into adjacent counties → overlaps
  "suburb of"      → does NOT imply touches — suburb may be separated from the main city → disjoint
  "surrounded by"  → may be touches (partial encirclement), not within (full containment)
  "enclave of"     → touches (shared boundary), NOT within (not interior)
  "across from"    → touches (water boundary), not within
  "separate from" → ambiguous; use OSM evidence and distance

Rules (follow all steps; never conclude solely from bbox):
1. DIRECTION CHECK (mandatory before answering):
   — "contains" means A encloses B; "within" means A is inside B.
   — Ask: does this verb apply A→B or B→A?
   — If the data shows B encloses A, the answer is "within", NOT "contains".

2. TEXTUAL QUALIFIER PRIORITY (override bbox signals when contradictory):
   — "most sides", "surrounded by" with incomplete encirclement → touches.
   — "partly in", "extends into", "part of population in" → overlaps.
   — "enclave of" → touches (shared boundary, not interior).
   — "across from" over water → touches (shared water boundary).
   — "separate from" alone is ambiguous; rely on OSM geometry and distance.

3. GEOMETRY TYPE CONSTRAINTS (use the OSM coordinates, bbox, and hierarchy below):
   — Two Polygons: if one is entirely inside the other → within (or contains, direction check).
   — LineString (river, road) with Polygon: if LineString traverses interior → crosses; if only touches boundary → touches.
   — "At the mouth of" / "upstream from" → check bbox and centroid distance; may be crosses or disjoint.
   - Use the OSM coordinates, bbox, and hierarchy below to reason topologically.  Do not guess.

4. BOUNDING BOX CAUTION (never conclude directly from bbox):
   — bbox overlap ≠ polygon overlap.  Polygons inside overlapping bboxes may still be disjoint.
   — bbox containment ≠ within/contains.  A country bbox contains almost all nearby entities.
   — Treat bbox as a clue, not proof.

5. ENTITY VALIDATION (warnings):
   — If the OSM geometry does NOT match the vernacular context (e.g., a village far from a city), do NOT use bbox or centroid distance; the geometry is unreliable for this relation.
   — Check the coordinates, bbox, and hierarchy below; if the entity appears far from the described context, ignore its geometry.

6. DISTANCE CHECK (use the centroid coordinates and distance below; do not rely on bbox alone):
   — Distance between centroids: 0.829 km — very close, likely touching or overlapping.
   — A distance of < 0.01 degrees (~1 km) is NOT definitive proof of disjoint; administrative boundaries often share exact edges with 0-meter gap.

7. 3-STEP CHECK for OVERLAPS (do this before concluding within or touches):
   Step 1: Is A completely inside B? -> within (not overlaps)
   Step 2: Is B completely inside A? -> contains (not overlaps)
   Step 3: Do A and B share SOME area but NEITHER fully contains the other? -> overlaps
   Strong overlaps signals: "partly in", "extends into", "straddles",
                            "part of population in", "county seat of" (city crosses county line).

8. Pick EXACTLY ONE predicate from: contains, within, touches, crosses, disjoint, overlaps.

9.
